LG1856	1	I CELEBRATE myself,
LG1856	2	And what I assume you shall assume,
LG1856	3	For every atom belonging to me, as good belongs to you.
LG1856	4	I loafe and invite my soul,
LG1856	5	I lean and loafe at my ease, observing a spear of summer grass.
LG1856	6	Houses and rooms are full of perfumes—the shelves are crowded with perfumes,
LG1856	7	I breathe the fragrance myself, and know it and like it,
LG1856	8	The distillation would intoxicate me also, but I shall not let it.
LG1856	9	The atmosphere is not a perfume, it has no taste of the distillation, it is odorless,
LG1856	10	It is for my mouth forever, I am in love with it,
LG1856	11	I will go to the bank by the wood, and become undisguised and naked,
LG1856	12	I am mad for it to be in contact with me.
LG1856	13	The smoke of my own breath,
LG1856	14	Echoes, ripples, buzzed whispers, love-root, silk-threadsilk-thread, crotch, vine,
LG1856	15	My respiration and inspiration, the beating of my heart, the passing of blood and air through my lungs,
LG1856	16	The sniff of green leaves and dry leaves, and of the shore and dark-colored sea-rocks, and of hay in the barn,
LG1856	17	The sound of the belched words of my voice, words loosed to the eddies of the wind,
LG1856	18	A few light kisses, a few embraces, a reaching around of arms,
LG1856	19	The play of shine and shade on the trees as the supple boughs wag,
LG1856	20	The delight alone, or in the rush of the streets, or along the fields and hill-sides,
LG1856	21	The feeling of health, the full-noon trill, the song of me rising from bed and meeting the sun.
LG1856	22	Have you reckoned a thousand acres much? have you reckoned the earth much?
LG1856	23	Have you practiced so long to learn to read?
LG1856	24	Have you felt so proud to get at the meaning of poems?
LG1856	25	Stop this day and night with me, and you shall possess the origin of all poems,
LG1856	26	You shall possess the good of the earth and sun — there are millions of suns left,
LG1856	27	You shall no longer take things at second or third hand, nor look through the eyes of the dead, nor feed on the spectres in books,
LG1856	28	You shall not look through my eyes either, nor take things from me,
LG1856	29	You shall listen to all sides, and filter them from yourself.
LG1856	30	I have heard what the talkers were talking, the talk of the beginning and the end,
LG1856	31	But I do not talk of the beginning or the end.
LG1856	32	There was never any more inception than there is now,
LG1856	33	Nor any more youth or age than there is now,
LG1856	34	And will never be any more perfection than there is now,
LG1856	35	Nor any more heaven or hell than there is now.
LG1856	36	Urge, and urge, and urge,
LG1856	37	Always the procreant urge of the world.
LG1856	38	Out of the dimness opposite equals advance — always substance and increase, always sex,
LG1856	39	Always a knit of identity, always distinction, always a breed of life.
LG1856	40	To elaborate is no avail—learned and unlearned feel that it is so.
LG1856	41	Sure as the most certain sure, plumb in the uprights, well entretied, braced in the beams,
LG1856	42	Stout as a horse, affectionate, haughty, electrical,
LG1856	43	I and this mystery here we stand.
LG1856	44	Clear and sweet is my soul, and clear and sweet is all that is not my soul.
LG1856	45	Lack one lacks both, and the unseen is proved by the seen,
LG1856	46	Till that becomes unseen, and receives proof in its turn.
LG1856	47	Showing the best and dividing it from the worst, age vexes age,
LG1856	48	Knowing the perfect fitness and equanimity of things, while they discuss I am silent, and go bathe and admire myself.
LG1856	49	Welcome is every organ and attribute of me, and of any man hearty and clean,
LG1856	50	Not an inch nor a particle of an inch is vile, and none shall be less familiar than the rest.
LG1856	51	I am satisfied—I see, dance, laugh, sing;
LG1856	52	As the hugging and loving Bed-fellow sleeps at my side through the night, and withdraws at the peep of the day,
LG1856	53	And leaves for me baskets covered with white towels, swelling the house with their plenty,
LG1856	54	Shall I postpone my acceptation and realization, and scream at my eyes,
LG1856	55	That they turn from gazing after and down the road,
LG1856	56	And forthwith cipher and show me to a cent,
LG1856	57	Exactly the contents of one, and exactly the con-tentscontents of two, and which is ahead?
LG1856	58	Trippers and askers surround me,
LG1856	59	People I meet—the effect upon me of my early life, of the ward and city I live in, of the nation,
LG1856	60	The latest news, discoveries, inventions, societies, authors old and new,
LG1856	61	My dinner, dress, associates, looks, work, compli-mentscompliments, dues,
LG1856	62	The real or fancied indifference of some man or woman I love,
LG1856	63	The sickness of one of my folks, or of myself, or ill-doing, or loss or lack of money, or depress-ionsdepressions or exaltations,
LG1856	64	They come to me days and nights and go from me again,
LG1856	65	But they are not the Me myself.
LG1856	66	Apart from the pulling and hauling stands what I am,
LG1856	67	Stands amused, complacent, compassionating, idle, unitary,
LG1856	68	Looks down, is erect, bends an arm on an impalpable certain rest,
LG1856	69	Looks with its side-curved head, curious what will come next,
LG1856	70	Both in and out of the game, and watching and wondering at it.
LG1856	71	Backward I see in my own days where I sweated through fog with linguists and contenders,
LG1856	72	I have no mockings or arguments—I witness and wait.
LG1856	73	I believe in you, my soul—the other I am must not abase itself to you,
LG1856	74	And you must not be abased to the other.
LG1856	75	Loafe with me on the grass, loose the stop from your throat,
LG1856	76	Not words, not music or rhyme I want—not cus-tomcustom or lecture, not even the best,
LG1856	77	Only the lull I like, the hum of your valved voice.
LG1856	78	I mind how we lay in June, such a transparent summer morning,
LG1856	79	You settled your head athwart my hips, and gently turned over upon me,
LG1856	80	And parted the shirt from my bosom-bone, and plunged your tongue to my bare-stript heart,
LG1856	81	And reached till you felt my beard, and reached till you held my feet.
LG1856	82	Swiftly arose and spread around me the peace and joy and knowledge that pass all the art and argument of the earth,
LG1856	83	And I know that the hand of God is the promise of my own,
LG1856	84	And I know that the spirit of God is the brother of my own,
LG1856	85	And that all the men ever born are also my bro-thersbrothers, and the women my sisters and lovers,
LG1856	86	And that a kelson of the creation is love,
LG1856	87	And limitless are leaves, stiff or drooping in the fields,
LG1856	88	And brown ants in the little wells beneath them,
LG1856	89	And mossy scabs of the worm-fence, heaped stones, elder, mullen, pokeweed.
LG1856	90	A child said, What is the grass? fetching it to me with full hands;
LG1856	91	How could I answer the child? I do not know what it is any more than he.
LG1856	92	I guess it must be the flag of my disposition, out of hopeful green stuff woven.
LG1856	93	Or I guess it is the handkerchief of the Lord,
LG1856	94	A scented gift and remembrancer, designedly dropped,
LG1856	95	Bearing the owner's name someway in the corners, that we may see and remark, and say Whose?
LG1856	96	Or I guess the grass is itself a child, the produced babe of the vegetation.
LG1856	97	Or I guess it is a uniform hieroglyphic,
LG1856	98	And it means, Sprouting alike in broad zones and narrow zones,
LG1856	99	Growing among black folks as among white,
LG1856	100	Kanuck, Tuckahoe, Congressman, Cuff, I give them the same, I receive them the same.
LG1856	101	And now it seems to me the beautiful uncut hair of graves.
LG1856	102	Tenderly will I use you, curling grass,
LG1856	103	It may be you transpire from the breasts of young men,
LG1856	104	It may be if I had known them I would have loved them,
LG1856	105	It may be you are from old people, and from women, and from offspring taken soon out of their mothers' laps,
LG1856	106	And here you are the mothers' laps.
LG1856	107	This grass is very dark to be from the white heads of old mothers,
LG1856	108	Darker than the colorless beards of old men,
LG1856	109	Dark to come from under the faint red roofs of mouths.
LG1856	110	O I perceive after all so many uttering tongues!
LG1856	111	And I perceive they do not come from the roofs of mouths for nothing.
LG1856	112	I wish I could translate the hints about the dead young men and women,
LG1856	113	And the hints about old men and mothers, and the offspring taken soon out of their laps.
LG1856	114	What do you think has become of the young and old men?
LG1856	115	And what do you think has become of the women and children?
LG1856	116	They are alive and well somewhere,
LG1856	117	The smallest sprout shows there is really no death,
LG1856	118	And if ever there was, it led forward life, and does not wait at the end to arrest it,
LG1856	119	And ceased the moment life appeared.
LG1856	120	All goes onward and outward—nothing collapses,
LG1856	121	And to die is different from what any one sup-posedsupposed, and luckier.
LG1856	122	Has any one supposed it lucky to be born?
LG1856	123	I hasten to inform him or her, it is just as lucky to die, and I know it.
LG1856	124	I pass death with the dying, and birth with the new-washed babe, and am not contained be-tweenbetween my hat and boots,
LG1856	125	And peruse manifold objects, no two alike, and every one good,
LG1856	126	The earth good, and the stars good, and their ad-junctsadjuncts all good.
LG1856	127	I am not an earth nor an adjunct of an earth,
LG1856	128	I am the mate and companion of people, all just as immortal and fathomless as myself;
LG1856	129	They do not know how immortal, but I know.
LG1856	130	Every kind for itself and its own—for me mine, male and female,
LG1856	131	For me those that have been boys and that love women,
LG1856	132	For me the man that is proud, and feels how it stings to be slighted,
LG1856	133	For me the sweetheart and the old maid—for me mothers and the mothers of mothers,
LG1856	134	For me lips that have smiled, eyes that have shed tears,
LG1856	135	For me children and the begetters of children.
LG1856	136	Who need be afraid of the merge?
LG1856	137	Undrape! you are not guilty to me, nor stale, nor discarded,
LG1856	138	I see through the broadcloth and gingham, whether or no,
LG1856	139	And am around, tenacious, acquisitive, tireless, and can never be shaken away.
LG1856	140	The little one sleeps in its cradle,
LG1856	141	I lift the gauze and look a long time, and silently brush away flies with my hand.
LG1856	142	The youngster and the red-faced girl turn aside up the bushy hill,
LG1856	143	I peeringly view them from the top.
LG1856	144	The suicide sprawls on the bloody floor of the bedroom,
LG1856	145	It is so—I witnessed the corpse—there the pistol had fallen.
LG1856	146	The blab of the pave, the tires of carts, sluff of boot-soles, talk of the promenaders,
LG1856	147	The heavy omnibus, the driver with his interrogat-inginterrogating thumb, the clank of the shod horses on the granite floor,
LG1856	148	The snow-sleighs, the clinking, shouted jokes, pelts of snow-balls,
LG1856	149	The hurrahs for popular favorites, the fury of roused mobs,
LG1856	150	The flap of the curtained litter, the sick man in-sideinside, borne to the hospital,
LG1856	151	The meeting of enemies, the sudden oath, the blows and fall,
LG1856	152	The excited crowd, the policeman with his star, quickly working his passage to the centre of the crowd,
LG1856	153	The impassive stones that receive and return so many echoes,
LG1856	154	The souls moving along—are they invisible, while the least of the stones is visible?
LG1856	155	What groans of over-fed or half-starved who fall sun-struck, or in fits,
LG1856	156	What exclamations of women taken suddenly, who hurry home and give birth to babes,
LG1856	157	What living and buried speech is always vibrating here, what howls restrained by decorum,
LG1856	158	Arrests of criminals, slights, adulterous offers made, acceptances, rejections with convex lips,
LG1856	159	I mind them or the resonance of them—I come and I depart.
LG1856	160	The big doors of the country-barn stand open and ready,
LG1856	161	The dried grass of the harvest-time loads the slow-drawn wagon,
LG1856	162	The clear light plays on the brown gray and green intertinged,
LG1856	163	The armfuls are packed to the sagging mow;
LG1856	164	I am there, I help, I came stretched atop of the load,
LG1856	165	I felt its soft jolts, one leg reclined on the other;
LG1856	166	I jump from the cross-beams and seize the clover and timothy,
LG1856	167	And roll head over heels, and tangle my hair full of wisps.
LG1856	168	Alone, far in the wilds and mountains, I hunt,
LG1856	169	Wandering, amazed at my own lightness and glee,
LG1856	170	In the late afternoon choosing a safe spot to pass the night,
LG1856	171	Kindling a fire and broiling the fresh-killed game,
LG1856	172	Soundly falling asleep on the gathered leaves, my dog and gun by my side.
LG1856	173	The Yankee clipper is under her three sky-sails, she cuts the sparkle and scud,
LG1856	174	My eyes settle the land—I bend at her prow or shout joyously from the deck.
LG1856	175	The boatmen and clam-diggers arose early and stopped for me,
LG1856	176	I tucked my trowser-ends in my boots and went and had a good time,
LG1856	177	You should have been with us that day round the chowder-kettle.
LG1856	178	I saw the marriage of the trapper in the open air in the far-west—the bride was a red girl,
LG1856	179	Her father and his friends sat near, cross-legged and dumbly smoking—they had moccasins to their feet and large thick blankets hanging from their shoulders,
LG1856	180	On a bank lounged the trapper, he was dressed mostly in skins, his luxuriant beard and curls protected his neck,
LG1856	181	One hand rested on his rifle, the other hand held firmly the wrist of the red girl,
LG1856	182	She had long eyelashes, her head was bare, her coarse straight locks descended upon her voluptuous limbs and reached to her feet.
LG1856	183	The runaway slave came to my house and stopped outside,
LG1856	184	I heard his motions crackling the twigs of the wood-pile,
LG1856	185	Through the swung half-door of the kitchen I saw him limpsy and weak,
LG1856	186	And went where he sat on a log, and led him in and assured him,
LG1856	187	And brought water and filled a tub for his sweated body and bruised feet,
LG1856	188	And gave him a room that entered from my own, and gave him some coarse clean clothes,
LG1856	189	And remember perfectly well his revolving eyes and his awkwardness,
LG1856	190	And remember putting plasters on the galls of his neck and ankles;
LG1856	191	He staid with me a week before he was recuper-atedrecuperated and passed north,
LG1856	192	I had him sit next me at table—my fire-lock leaned in the corner.
LG1856	193	Twenty-eight young men bathe by the shore,
LG1856	194	Twenty-eight young men, and all so friendly,
LG1856	195	Twenty-eight years of womanly life, and all so lonesome.
LG1856	196	She owns the fine house by the rise of the bank,
LG1856	197	She hides, handsome and richly drest, aft the blinds of the window.
LG1856	198	Which of the young men does she like the best?
LG1856	199	Ah, the homeliest of them is beautiful to her.
LG1856	200	Where are you off to, lady? for I see you,
LG1856	201	You splash in the water there, yet stay stock still in your room.
LG1856	202	Dancing and laughing along the beach came the twenty-ninth bather,
LG1856	203	The rest did not see her, but she saw them and loved them.
LG1856	204	The beards of the young men glistened with wet, it ran from their long hair,
LG1856	205	Little streams passed all over their bodies.
LG1856	206	An unseen hand also passed over their bodies,
LG1856	207	It descended tremblingly from their temples and ribs.
LG1856	208	The young men float on their backs, their white bellies bulge to the sun, they do not ask who seizes fast to them,
LG1856	209	They do not know who puffs and declines with pendant and bending arch,
LG1856	210	They do not think whom they souse with spray.
LG1856	211	The butcher-boy puts off his killing-clothes, or sharpens his knife at the stall in the mar-ketmarket,
LG1856	212	I loiter, enjoying his repartee and his shuffle and break-down.
LG1856	213	Blacksmiths with grimed and hairy chests environ the anvil,
LG1856	214	Each has his main-sledge—they are all out — there is a great heat in the fire.
LG1856	215	From the cinder-strewed threshold I follow their movements,
LG1856	216	The lithe sheer of their waists plays even with their massive arms,
LG1856	217	Overhand the hammers roll, overhand so slow, overhand so sure,
LG1856	218	They do not hasten, each man hits in his place.
LG1856	219	The negro holds firmly the reins of his four horses, the block swags underneath on its tied-over chain,
LG1856	220	The negro that drives the huge dray of the stone-yardstoneyard, steady and tall he stands poised on one leg on the string-piece,
LG1856	221	His blue shirt exposes his ample neck and breast, and loosens over his hip-band,
LG1856	222	His glance is calm and commanding, he tosses the slouch of his hat away from his forehead,
LG1856	223	The sun falls on his crispy hair and moustache, falls on the black of his polish'd and perfect limbs.
LG1856	224	I behold the picturesque giant and love him, and I do not stop there,
LG1856	225	I go with the team also.
LG1856	226	In me the caresser of life wherever moving, back-wardbackward as well as forward slueing,
LG1856	227	To niches aside and junior bending.
LG1856	228	Oxen that rattle the yoke or halt in the shade! what is that you express in your eyes?
LG1856	229	It seems to me more than all the print I have read in my life.
LG1856	230	My tread scares the wood-drake and wood-duck, on my distant and day-long ramble,
LG1856	231	They rise together, they slowly circle around;
LG1856	232	I believe in those winged purposes,
LG1856	233	And acknowledge, red, yellow, white, playing within me,
LG1856	234	And consider green and violet, and the tufted crown, intentional,
LG1856	235	And do not call the tortoise unworthy because she is not something else,
LG1856	236	And the mocking-bird in the swamp never studied the gamut, yet trills pretty well to me,
LG1856	237	And the look of the bay mare shames silliness out of me.
LG1856	238	The wild gander leads his flock through the cool night,
LG1856	239	Ya-honk! he says, and sounds it down to me like an invitation;
LG1856	240	The pert may suppose it meaningless, but I listen close,
LG1856	241	I find its purpose and place up there toward the November sky.
LG1856	242	The sharp-hoofed moose of the north, the cat on the house-sill, the chickadee, the prairie-dog,
LG1856	243	The litter of the grunting sow as they tug at her teats,
LG1856	244	The brood of the turkey-hen, and she with her half-spread wings,
LG1856	245	I see in them and myself the same old law.
LG1856	246	The press of my foot to the earth springs a hun-dredhundred affections,
LG1856	247	They scorn the best I can do to relate them.
LG1856	248	I am enamoured of growing outdoors,
LG1856	249	Of men that live among cattle, or taste of the ocean or woods,
LG1856	250	Of the builders and steerers of ships, of the wield-erswielders of axes and mauls, of the drivers of horses,
LG1856	251	I can eat and sleep with them week in and week out.
LG1856	252	What is commonest, cheapest, nearest, easiest, is Me,
LG1856	253	Me going in for my chances, spending for vast returns,
LG1856	254	Adorning myself to bestow myself on the first that will take me,
LG1856	255	Not asking the sky to come down to my good-will,
LG1856	256	Scattering it freely forever.
LG1856	257	The pure contralto sings in the organ-loft,

LG1856	322	The living sleep for their time, the dead sleep for their time.
LG1856	323	The old husband sleeps by his wife, and the young husband sleeps by his wife;
LG1856	324	And these one and all tend inward to me, and I tend outward to them,
LG1856	325	And such as it is to be of these, more or less, I am.
LG1856	326	I am of old and young, of the foolish as much as the wise,
LG1856	327	Regardless of others, ever regardful of others,
LG1856	328	Maternal as well as paternal, a child as well as a man,
LG1856	329	Stuffed with the stuff that is coarse, and stuffed with the stuff that is fine,
LG1856	330	One of the great nation, the nation of many nations, the smallest the same, the largest the same,
LG1856	331	A southerner soon as a northerner, a planter non-chalantnonchalant and hospitable,
LG1856	332	A Yankee bound my own way, ready for trade, my joints the limberest joints on earth and the sternest joints on earth,
LG1856	333	A Kentuckian walking the vale of the Elkhorn in my deer-skin leggings,
LG1856	334	A boatman over lakes or bays, or along coasts — a Hoosier, Badger, Buckeye,
LG1856	335	A Louisianian or Georgian, a Poke-easy from sand-hills and pines,
LG1856	336	At home on Canadian snow-shoes, or up in the bush, or with fishermen off Newfoundland,
LG1856	337	At home in the fleet of ice-boats, sailing with the rest, and tacking,
LG1856	338	At home on the hills of Vermont, or in the woods of Maine, or the Texan ranch,
LG1856	339	Comrade of Californians, comrade of free north-westernersnorthwesterners, loving their big proportions.
LG1856	340	Comrade of raftsmen and coalmen, comrade of all who shake hands and welcome to drink and meat,
LG1856	341	A learner with the simplest, a teacher of the thoughtfulest,
LG1856	342	A novice beginning, experient of myriads of sea-sonsseasons,
LG1856	343	Of every hue, trade, rank, of every caste and re-ligionreligion,
LG1856	344	Not merely of the New World, but of Africa, Europe, Asia—a wandering savage,
LG1856	345	A farmer, mechanic, artist, gentleman, sailor, lover, quaker,
LG1856	346	A prisoner, fancy-man, rowdy, lawyer, physician, priest.
LG1856	347	I resist anything better than my own diversity,
LG1856	348	And breathe the air, and leave plenty after me,
LG1856	349	And am not stuck up, and am in my place.
LG1856	350	The moth and the fish-eggs are in their place,
LG1856	351	The suns I see, and the suns I cannot see, are in their place,
LG1856	352	The palpable is in its place, and the impalpable is in its place.
LG1856	353	These are the thoughts of all men in all ages and lands, they are not original with me,
LG1856	354	If they are not yours as much as mine, they are nothing, or next to nothing,
LG1856	355	If they do not enclose everything, they are next to nothing,
LG1856	356	If they are not the riddle and the untying of the riddle, they are nothing,
LG1856	357	If they are not just as close as they are distant, they are nothing.
LG1856	358	This is the grass that grows wherever the land is and the water is,
LG1856	359	This is the common air that bathes the globe.
LG1856	360	This is the breath of laws, songs, behaviour,
LG1856	361	This is the tasteless water of souls, this is the true sustenance,
LG1856	362	It is for the illiterate, it is for the judges of the supreme court, it is for the federal capitol and the state capitols,
LG1856	363	It is for the admirable communes of literats, composers, singers, lecturers, engineers, sa-vanssavans,
LG1856	364	It is for the endless races of work-people, farm-ersfarmers, seamen.
LG1856	365	These are trills of thousands of clear cornets, screams of octave flutes, strike of triangles.
LG1856	366	I play not a march for victors only, I play great marches for conquered and slain persons.
LG1856	367	Have you heard that it was good to gain the day?
LG1856	368	I also say it is good to fall—battles are lost in the same spirit in which they are won.
LG1856	369	I beat triumphal drums for the dead, I blow through my embouchures my loudest and gayest music to them,
LG1856	370	Vivas to those who have failed! and to those whose war-vessels sank in the sea! and those themselves who sank in the sea!
LG1856	371	And to all generals that lost engagements! and all overcome heroes! and the numberless un-knownunknown heroes, equal to the greatest heroes known!
LG1856	372	This is the meal pleasantly set, this is the meat and drink for natural hunger,
LG1856	373	It is for the wicked just the same as the righteous —I make appointments with all,
LG1856	374	I will not have a single person slighted or left away,
LG1856	375	The kept-woman, sponger, thief, are hereby in-vitedinvited—the heavy-lipped slave is invited, the venerealee is invited,
LG1856	376	There shall be no difference between them and the rest.
LG1856	377	This is the press of a bashful hand, this is the float and odor of hair,
LG1856	378	This is the touch of my lips to yours, this is the murmur of yearning,
LG1856	379	This is the far-off depth and height reflecting my own face,
LG1856	380	This is the thoughtful merge of myself, and the outlet again.
LG1856	381	Do you guess I have some intricate purpose?
LG1856	382	Well, I have—for the April rain has, and the mica on the side of a rock has.
LG1856	383	Do you take it I would astonish?
LG1856	384	Does the daylight astonish? Does the early red-startredstart, twittering through the woods?
LG1856	385	Do I astonish more than they?
LG1856	386	This hour I tell things in confidence,
LG1856	387	I might not tell everybody, but I will tell you.
LG1856	388	Who goes there! hankering, gross, mystical, nude?
LG1856	389	How is it I extract strength from the beef I eat?
LG1856	390	What is a man anyhow? What am I? What are you?
LG1856	391	All I mark as my own, you shall offset it with your own,
LG1856	392	Else it were time lost listening to me.
LG1856	393	I do not snivel that snivel the world over,
LG1856	394	That months are vacuums, and the ground but wallow and filth,
LG1856	395	That life is a suck and a sell, and nothing remains at the end but threadbare crape and tears.
LG1856	396	Whimpering and truckling fold with powders for invalids, conformity goes to the fourth-removedfourth-removed,
LG1856	397	I cock my hat as I please, indoors or out.
LG1856	398	Shall I pray? Shall I venerate and be cere-moniousceremonious?
LG1856	399	I have pried through the strata, analyzed to a hair,
LG1856	400	Counselled with doctors, calculated close, found no sweeter fat than sticks to my own bones.
LG1856	401	In all people I see myself—none more, not one a barleycorn less,
LG1856	402	And the good or bad I say of myself I say of them.
LG1856	403	And I know I am solid and sound,
LG1856	404	To me the converging objects of the universe per-petuallyperpetually flow,
LG1856	405	All are written to me, and I must get what the writing means.
LG1856	406	I know I am deathless,
LG1856	407	I know this orbit of mine cannot be swept by a carpenter's compass,
LG1856	408	I know I shall not pass like a child's carlacue cut with a burnt stick at night.
LG1856	409	I know I am august,
LG1856	410	I do not trouble my spirit to vindicate itself or be understood,
LG1856	411	I see that the elementary laws never apologize,
LG1856	412	I reckon I behave no prouder than the level I plant my house by, after all.
LG1856	413	I exist as I am, that is enough,
LG1856	414	If no other in the world be aware, I sit content,
LG1856	415	And if each and all be aware, I sit content.
LG1856	416	One world is aware, and by far the largest to me, and that is myself,
LG1856	417	And whether I come to my own today, or in ten thousand or ten million years,
LG1856	418	I can cheerfully take it now, or with equal cheer-fulnesscheerfulness I can wait.
LG1856	419	My foothold is tenoned and mortised in granite,
LG1856	420	I laugh at what you call dissolution,
LG1856	421	And I know the amplitude of time.
LG1856	422	I am the poet of the body,
LG1856	423	And I am the poet of the soul.
LG1856	424	The pleasures of heaven are with me, and the pains of hell are with me,
LG1856	425	The first I graft and increase upon myself, the latter I translate into a new tongue.
LG1856	426	I am the poet of the woman the same as the man,
LG1856	427	And I say it is as great to be a woman as to be a man,
LG1856	428	And I say there is nothing greater than the mother of men.
LG1856	429	I chant the chant of dilation or pride,
LG1856	430	We have had ducking and deprecating about enough,
LG1856	431	I show that size is only development.
LG1856	432	Have you outstript the rest? are you the President?
LG1856	433	It is a trifle—they will more than arrive there every one, and still pass on.
LG1856	434	I am he that walks with the tender and growing night,
LG1856	435	I call to the earth and sea, half-held by the night.
LG1856	436	Press close, bare-bosomed night! press close, magnetic, nourishing night!
LG1856	437	Night of south winds! night of the large few stars!
LG1856	438	Still, nodding night! mad, naked, summer night!
LG1856	439	Smile, O voluptuous, cool-breathed earth!
LG1856	440	Earth of the slumbering and liquid trees!
LG1856	441	Earth of departed sunset! earth of the moun-tainsmountains, misty-topt!
LG1856	442	Earth of the vitreous pour of the full moon, just tinged with blue!
LG1856	443	Earth of shine and dark, mottling the tide of the river!
LG1856	444	Earth of the limpid gray of clouds, brighter and clearer for my sake!
LG1856	445	Far-swooping elbowed earth! rich, apple-blos-somedapple-blossomed earth!
LG1856	446	Smile, for your lover comes!
LG1856	447	Prodigal, you have given me love! therefore I to you give love!
LG1856	448	O unspeakable passionate love!
LG1856	449	Thruster holding me tight, and that I hold tight!
LG1856	450	We hurt each other as the bridegroom and the bride hurt each other.
LG1856	451	You sea! I resign myself to you also, I guess what you mean,
LG1856	452	I behold from the beach your crooked inviting fingers,
LG1856	453	I believe you refuse to go back without feeling of me,
LG1856	454	We must have a turn together—I undress — hurry me out of sight of the land,
LG1856	455	Cushion me soft, rock me in billowy drowse,
LG1856	456	Dash me with amorous wet, I can repay you.
LG1856	457	Sea of stretched ground-swells!
LG1856	458	Sea breathing broad and convulsive breaths!
LG1856	459	Sea of the brine of life! sea of unshovelled and always-ready graves!
LG1856	460	Howler and scooper of storms! capricious and dainty sea!
LG1856	461	I am integral with you—I too am of one phase, and of all phases.
LG1856	462	Partaker of influx and efflux, extoller of hate and conciliation,
LG1856	463	Extoller of amies, and those that sleep in each others' arms.
LG1856	464	I am he attesting sympathy,
LG1856	465	Shall I make my list of things in the house, and skip the house that supports them?
LG1856	466	I am the poet of commonsense, and of the demon-strabledemonstrable, and of immortality,
LG1856	467	And am not the poet of goodness only—I do not decline to be the poet of wickedness also.
LG1856	468	Washes and razors for foofoos—for me freckles and a bristling beard.
LG1856	469	What blurt is this about virtue and about vice?
LG1856	470	Evil propels me, and reform of evil propels me — I stand indifferent,
LG1856	471	My gait is no fault-finder's or rejecter's gait,
LG1856	472	I moisten the roots of all that has grown.
LG1856	473	Did you fear some scrofula out of the unflagging pregnancy?
LG1856	474	Did you guess the celestial laws are yet to be worked over and rectified?
LG1856	475	I step up to say that what we do is right, and what we affirm is right, and some is only the ore of right,
LG1856	476	Witnesses of us, one side a balance, and the anti-podalantipodal side a balance,
LG1856	477	Soft doctrine as steady help as stable doctrine,
LG1856	478	Thoughts and deeds of the present, our rouse and early start.
LG1856	479	This minute that comes to me over the past de-cillionsdecillions,
LG1856	480	There is no better than it and now.
LG1856	481	What behaved well in the past, or behaves well today, is not such a wonder,
LG1856	482	The wonder is always and always how can there be a mean man or an infidel.
LG1856	483	Endless unfolding of words of ages!
LG1856	484	And mine a word of the modern—a word en-masseen-masse,
LG1856	485	A word of the faith that never balks,
LG1856	486	One time as good as another time—here or henceforward it is all the same to me,
LG1856	487	A word of reality, materialism first and last im-bueingimbueing.
LG1856	488	Hurrah for positive science! long live exact demonstration!
LG1856	489	Fetch stonecrop, mix it with cedar and branches of lilac,
LG1856	490	This is the lexicographer, this the chemist, this made a grammar of the old cartouches,
LG1856	491	These mariners put the ship through dangerous unknown seas,
LG1856	492	This is the geologist, this works with the scalpel, and this is a mathematician.
LG1856	493	Gentlemen, I receive you and attach and clasp hands with you,
LG1856	494	The facts are useful and real—they are not my dwelling—I enter by them to an area of the dwelling.
LG1856	495	I am less the reminder of property or qualities, and more the reminder of life,
LG1856	496	And go on the square for my own sake and for others' sakes,
LG1856	497	And make short account of neuters and geldings, and favor men and women fully equipped,
LG1856	498	And beat the gong of revolt, and stop with fugi-tivesfugitives and them that plot and conspire.
LG1856	499	Walt Whitman, an American, one of the roughs, a kosmos,
LG1856	500	Disorderly, fleshy, sensual, eating, drinking, breed-ingbreeding,
LG1856	501	No sentimentalist, no stander above men and wo-menwomen, or apart from them—no more modest than immodest.
LG1856	502	Unscrew the locks from the doors!
LG1856	503	Unscrew the doors themselves from their jambs!
LG1856	504	Whoever degrades another degrades me, and whatever is done or said returns at last to me,
LG1856	505	And whatever I do or say, I also return.
LG1856	506	Through me the afflatus surging and surging — through me the current and index.
LG1856	507	I speak the pass-word primeval, I give the sign of democracy,
LG1856	508	By God! I will accept nothing which all cannot have their counterpart of on the same terms.
LG1856	509	Through me many long dumb voices,
LG1856	510	Voices of the interminable generations of slaves,
LG1856	511	Voices of prostitutes, and of deformed persons,
LG1856	512	Voices of the diseased and despairing, and of thieves and dwarfs,
LG1856	513	Voices of cycles of preparation and accretion,
LG1856	514	And of the threads that connect the stars, and of wombs, and of the fatherstuff,
LG1856	515	And of the rights of them the others are down upon,
LG1856	516	Of the trivial, flat, foolish, despised,
LG1856	517	Fog in the air, beetles rolling balls of dung.
LG1856	518	Through me forbidden voices,
LG1856	519	Voices of sexes and lusts—voices veiled, and I remove the veil,
LG1856	520	Voices indecent, by me clarified and transfigured.
LG1856	521	I do not press my finger across my mouth,
LG1856	522	I keep as delicate around the bowels as around the head and heart,
LG1856	523	Copulation is no more rank to me than death is.
LG1856	524	I believe in the flesh and the appetites,
LG1856	525	Seeing, hearing, feeling, are miracles, and each part and tag of me is a miracle.
LG1856	526	Divine am I inside and out, and I make holy whatever I touch or am touched from,
LG1856	527	The scent of these arm-pits is aroma finer than prayer,
LG1856	528	This head is more than churches, bibles, creeds.
LG1856	529	If I worship any particular thing, it shall be some of the spread of my own body,
LG1856	530	Translucent mould of me, it shall be you!
LG1856	531	Shaded ledges and rests, firm masculine coulter, it shall be you!
LG1856	532	Whatever goes to the tilth of me, it shall be you!
LG1856	533	You my rich blood! your milky stream, pale strip-pingsstrippings of my life!
LG1856	534	Breast that presses against other breasts, it shall be you!
LG1856	535	My brain, it shall be your occult convolutions!
LG1856	536	Root of washed sweet-flag, timorous pond-snipe, nest of guarded duplicate eggs, it shall be you!
LG1856	537	Mixed tussled hay of head, beard, brawn, it shall be you!
LG1856	538	Trickling sap of maple, fibre of manly wheat, it shall be you!
LG1856	539	Sun so generous, it shall be you!
LG1856	540	Vapors lighting and shading my face, it shall be you!
LG1856	541	You sweaty brooks and dews, it shall be you!
LG1856	542	Winds whose soft-tickling genitals rub against me, it shall be you!
LG1856	543	Broad muscular fields, branches of live-oak, loving lounger in my winding paths, it shall be you!
LG1856	544	Hands I have taken, face I have kissed, mortal I have ever touched, it shall be you!
LG1856	545	I dote on myself, there is that lot of me, and all so luscious,
LG1856	546	Each moment, and whatever happens, thrills me with joy.
LG1856	547	I cannot tell how my ankles bend, nor whence the cause of my faintest wish,
LG1856	548	Nor the cause of the friendship I emit, nor the cause of the friendship I take again.
LG1856	549	To walk up my stoop is unaccountable, I pause to consider if it really be,
LG1856	550	That I eat and drink is spectacle enough for the great authors and schools,
LG1856	551	A morning-glory at my window satisfies me more than the metaphysics of books.
LG1856	552	To behold the day-break!
LG1856	553	The little light fades the immense and diaphanous shadows,
LG1856	554	The air tastes good to my palate.
LG1856	555	Hefts of the moving world at innocent gambols, silently rising, freshly exuding,
LG1856	556	Scooting obliquely high and low.
LG1856	557	Something I cannot see puts upward libidinous prongs,
LG1856	558	Seas of bright juice suffuse heaven.
LG1856	559	The earth by the sky staid with, the daily close of their junction,
LG1856	560	The heaved challenge from the east that moment over my head,
LG1856	561	The mocking taunt, See then whether you shall be master!
LG1856	562	Dazzling and tremendous, how quick the sun-rise would kill me,
LG1856	563	If I could not now and always send sun-rise out of me.
LG1856	564	We also ascend dazzling and tremendous as the sun,
LG1856	565	We found our own, my soul, in the calm and cool of the day-break.
LG1856	566	My voice goes after what my eyes cannot reach,
LG1856	567	With the twirl of my tongue I encompass worlds, and volumes of worlds.
LG1856	568	Speech is the twin of my vision, it is unequal to measure itself.
LG1856	569	It provokes me forever,
LG1856	570	It says sarcastically, Walt, you understand enough, why don't you let it out then?
LG1856	571	Come now, I will not be tantalized, you conceive too much of articulation.
LG1856	572	Do you not know how the buds beneath are folded?
LG1856	573	Waiting in gloom, protected by frost,
LG1856	574	The dirt receding before my prophetical screams,
LG1856	575	I underlying causes, to balance them at last,
LG1856	576	My knowledge my live parts, it keeping tally with the meaning of things,
LG1856	577	Happiness, which, whoever hears me, let him or her set out in search of this day.
LG1856	578	My final merit I refuse you—I refuse putting from me the best I am.
LG1856	579	Encompass worlds, but never try to encompass me,
LG1856	580	I crowd your noisiest talk by looking toward you.
LG1856	581	Writing and talk do not prove me,
LG1856	582	I carry the plenum of proof, and every thing else, in my face,
LG1856	583	With the hush of my lips I confound the topmost skeptic.
LG1856	584	I think I will do nothing for a long time but listen,
LG1856	585	To accrue what I hear into myself, to let sounds contribute toward me.
LG1856	586	I hear bravuras of birds, bustle of growing wheat, gossip of flames, clack of sticks cooking my meals.
LG1856	587	I hear the sound I love, the sound of the human voice,
LG1856	588	I hear all sounds as they are tuned to their uses, sounds of the city and sounds out of the city, sounds of the day and night,
LG1856	589	Talkative young ones to those that like them, the recitative of fish-pedlars and fruit-pedlars, the loud laugh of work-people at their meals,
LG1856	590	The angry base of disjointed friendship, the faint tones of the sick,
LG1856	591	The judge with hands tight to the desk, his shaky lips pronouncing a death-sentence,
LG1856	592	The heave'e'yo of stevedores unlading ships by the wharves, the refrain of the anchor-lifters,
LG1856	593	The ring of alarm-bells, the cry of fire, the whirr of swift-streaking engines and hose-cartshose-carts, with premonitory tinkles and colored lights,
LG1856	594	The steam-whistle, the solid roll of the train of approaching cars,
LG1856	595	The slow-march played at night at the head of the association,
LG1856	596	They go to guard some corpse, the flag-tops are draped with black muslin.
LG1856	597	I hear the violincello or man's heart's complaint,
LG1856	598	I hear the keyed cornet, it glides quickly in through my ears, it shakes mad-sweet pangs through my belly and breast.
LG1856	599	I hear the chorus, it is a grand-opera—this in-deedindeed is music!
LG1856	600	A tenor large and fresh as the creation fills me,
LG1856	601	The orbic flex of his mouth is pouring and filling me full.
LG1856	602	I hear the trained soprano, she convulses me like the climax of my love-grip,
LG1856	603	The orchestra wrenches such ardors from me, I did not know I possessed them,
LG1856	604	It throbs me to gulps of the farthest down horror,
LG1856	605	It sails me, I dab with bare feet, they are licked by the indolent waves,
LG1856	606	I am exposed, cut by bitter and poisoned hail,
LG1856	607	Steeped amid honeyed morphine, my windpipe squeezed in the fakes of death,
LG1856	608	Let up again to feel the puzzle of puzzles,
LG1856	609	And that we call Being.
LG1856	610	To be in any form, what is that?
LG1856	611	If nothing lay more developed, the quahaug in its callous shell were enough.
LG1856	612	Mine is no callous shell,
LG1856	613	I have instant conductors all over me, whether I pass or stop,
LG1856	614	They seize every object and lead it harmlessly through me.
LG1856	615	I merely stir, press, feel with my fingers, and am happy,
LG1856	616	To touch my person to some one else's is about as much as I can stand.
LG1856	617	Is this then a touch? quivering me to a new identity,
LG1856	618	Flames and ether making a rush for my veins,
LG1856	619	Treacherous tip of me reaching and crowding to help them,
LG1856	620	My flesh and blood playing out lightning to strike what is hardly different from myself,
LG1856	621	On all sides prurient provokers stiffening my limbs,
LG1856	622	Straining the udder of my heart for its withheld drip,
LG1856	623	Behaving licentious toward me, taking no denial,
LG1856	624	Depriving me of my best, as for a purpose,
LG1856	625	Unbuttoning my clothes, holding me by the bare waist,
LG1856	626	Deluding my confusion with the calm of the sun-light and pasture-fields,
LG1856	627	Immodestly sliding the fellow-senses away,
LG1856	628	They bribed to swap off with touch, and go and graze at the edges of me,
LG1856	629	No consideration, no regard for my draining strength or my anger,
LG1856	630	Fetching the rest of the herd around to enjoy them awhile,
LG1856	631	Then all uniting to stand on a head-land and worry me.
LG1856	632	The sentries desert every other part of me,
LG1856	633	They have left me helpless to a red marauder,
LG1856	634	They all come to the head-land, to witness and assist against me.
LG1856	635	I am given up by traitors!
LG1856	636	I talk wildly, I have lost my wits, I and nobody else am the greatest traitor,
LG1856	637	I went myself first to the head-land, my own hands carried me there.
LG1856	638	You villain touch! what are you doing? my breath is tight in its throat,
LG1856	639	Unclench your floodgates! you are too much for me.
LG1856	640	Blind, loving, wrestling touch! sheathed, hooded, sharp-toothed touch!
LG1856	641	Did it make you ache so, leaving me?
LG1856	642	Parting, tracked by arriving—perpetual payment of the perpetual loan,
LG1856	643	Rich showering rain, and recompense richer after-wardafterward.
LG1856	644	Sprouts take and accumulate—stand by the curb prolific and vital,
LG1856	645	Landscapes, projected, masculine, full-sized, golden.
LG1856	646	All truths wait in all things,
LG1856	647	They neither hasten their own delivery, nor resist it,
LG1856	648	They do not need the obstetric forceps of the surgeon,
LG1856	649	The insignificant is as big to me as any,
LG1856	650	What is less or more than a touch?
LG1856	651	Logic and sermons never convince,
LG1856	652	The damp of the night drives deeper into my soul.
LG1856	653	Only what proves itself to every man and woman is so,
LG1856	654	Only what nobody denies is so.
LG1856	655	A minute and a drop of me settle my brain,
LG1856	656	I believe the soggy clods shall become lovers and lamps,
LG1856	657	And a compend of compends is the meat of a man or woman,
LG1856	658	And a summit and flower there is the feeling they have for each other,
LG1856	659	And they are to branch boundlessly out of that lesson until it becomes omnific,
LG1856	660	And until every one shall delight us, and we them.
LG1856	661	I believe a leaf of grass is no less than the journey-workjourneywork of the stars,
LG1856	662	And the pismire is equally perfect, and a grain of sand, and the egg of the wren,
LG1856	663	And the tree-toad is a chef-d'ouvre for the highest,
LG1856	664	And the running blackberry would adorn the parlors of heaven,
LG1856	665	And the narrowest hinge in my hand puts to scorn all machinery,
LG1856	666	And the cow crunching with depressed head sur-passessurpasses any statue,
LG1856	667	And a mouse is miracle enough to stagger sex-tillionssextillions of infidels,
LG1856	668	And I could come every afternoon of my life to look at the farmer's girl boiling her iron tea-kettletea-kettle and baking short-cake.
LG1856	669	I find I incorporate gneiss, coal, long-threaded moss, fruits, grains, esculent roots,
LG1856	670	And am stucco'd with quadrupeds and birds all over,
LG1856	671	And have distanced what is behind me for good reasons,
LG1856	672	And call any thing close again, when I desire it.
LG1856	673	In vain the speeding or shyness,
LG1856	674	In vain the plutonic rocks send their old heat against my approach,
LG1856	675	In vain the mastadon retreats beneath its own powdered bones,
LG1856	676	In vain objects stand leagues off, and assume manifold shapes,
LG1856	677	In vain the ocean settling in hollows, and the great monsters lying low,
LG1856	678	In vain the buzzard houses herself with the sky,
LG1856	679	In vain the snake slides through the creepers and logs,
LG1856	680	In vain the elk takes to the inner passes of the woods,
LG1856	681	In vain the razor-billed auk sails far north to Labrador,
LG1856	682	I follow quickly, I ascend to the nest in the fissure of the cliff.
LG1856	683	I think I could turn and live with animals, they are so placid and self-contained,
LG1856	684	I stand and look at them sometimes half the day long.
LG1856	685	They do not sweat and whine about their condi-tioncondition,
LG1856	686	They do not lie awake in the dark and weep for their sins,
LG1856	687	They do not make me sick discussing their duty to God,
LG1856	688	No one is dissatisfied, not one is demented with the mania of owning things,
LG1856	689	Not one kneels to another, nor to his kind that lived thousands of years ago,
LG1856	690	Not one is respectable or industrious over the whole earth.
LG1856	691	So they show their relations to me, and I accept them,
LG1856	692	They bring me tokens of myself, they evince them plainly in their possession.
LG1856	693	I do not know where they got those tokens,
LG1856	694	I may have passed that way untold times ago and negligently dropt them,
LG1856	695	Myself moving forward then and now and forever,
LG1856	696	Gathering and showing more always and with velocity,
LG1856	697	Infinite and omnigenous, and the like of these among them,
LG1856	698	Not too exclusive toward the reachers of my re-membrancersremembrancers,
LG1856	699	Picking out here one that I love, choosing to go with him on brotherly terms.
LG1856	700	A gigantic beauty of a stallion, fresh and respon-siveresponsive to my caresses,
LG1856	701	Head high in the forehead, wide between the ears,
LG1856	702	Limbs glossy and supple, tail dusting the ground,
LG1856	703	Eyes well apart, full of sparkling wickedness, ears finely cut, flexibly moving.
LG1856	704	His nostrils dilate, my heels embrace him, his well-built limbs tremble with pleasure, we speed around and return.
LG1856	705	I but use you a moment, then I resign you stal-lionstallion, do not need your paces, out-gallop them,
LG1856	706	Myself, as I stand or sit, passing faster than you.
LG1856	707	Swift wind! space! my soul! now I know it is true, what I guessed at,
LG1856	708	What I guessed when I loafed on the grass,
LG1856	709	What I guessed while I lay alone in my bed, and again as I walked the beach under the paling stars of the morning.
LG1856	710	My ties and ballasts leave me—I travel, I sail, my elbows rest in the sea-gaps,
LG1856	711	I skirt the sierras, my palms cover continents,
LG1856	712	I am afoot with my vision.
LG1856	713	By the city's quadrangular houses, in log-huts, camping with lumber-men,
LG1856	714	Along the ruts of the turnpike, along the dry gulch and rivulet bed,

LG1856	718	Where the panther walks to and fro on a limb overhead, where the buck turns furiously at the hunter,

LG1856	808	We are approaching some great battle-field in which we are soon to be engaged,

LG1856	814	My voice is the wife's voice, the screech by the rail of the stairs,
LG1856	815	They fetch my man's body up, dripping and drowned.
LG1856	816	I understand the large hearts of heroes,
LG1856	817	The courage of present times and all times,
LG1856	818	How the skipper saw the crowded and rudderless wreck of the steam-ship, and death chasing it up and down the storm,
LG1856	819	How he knuckled tight, and gave not back one inch, and was faithful of days and faithful of nights,

LG1856	825	I am the man, I suffered, I was there.
LG1856	826	The disdain and calmness of martyrs,
LG1856	827	The mother, condemned for a witch, burnt with dry wood, her children gazing on,

LG1856	863	Hear now the tale of a jet-black sunrise,
LG1856	864	Hear of the murder in cold-blood of four hundred and twelve young men.
LG1856	865	Retreating, they had formed in a hollow square, with their baggage for breast-works,
LG1856	866	Nine hundred lives out of the surrounding enemy's, nine times their number, was the price they took in advance,
LG1856	867	Their colonel was wounded and their ammunition gone,
LG1856	868	They treated for an honorable capitulation, re-ceivedreceived writing and seal, gave up their arms, marched back prisoners of war.
LG1856	869	They were the glory of the race of rangers,
LG1856	870	Matchless with horse, rifle, song, supper, court-shipcourtship,
LG1856	871	Large, turbulent, brave, handsome, generous, proud, affectionate,
LG1856	872	Bearded, sunburnt, dressed in the free costume of hunters,
LG1856	873	Not a single one over thirty years of age.
LG1856	874	The second Sunday morning they were brought out in squads and massacred—it was beauti-fulbeautiful early summer,
LG1856	875	The work commenced about five o'clock and was over by eight.
LG1856	876	None obeyed the command to kneel,
LG1856	877	Some made a mad and helpless rush, some stood stark and straight,

LG1856	881	These were dispatched with bayonets, or battered with the blunts of muskets,
LG1856	882	A youth not seventeen years old seized his assas-sinassassin, till two more came to release him,

LG1856	885	That is the tale of the murder of the four hun-dredhundred and twelve young men,
LG1856	886	And that was a jet-black sunrise.
LG1856	887	Did you read in the sea-books of the old-fashioned frigate-fight?
LG1856	888	Did you learn who won by the light of the moon and stars?
LG1856	889	Our foe was no skulk in his ship, I tell you,
LG1856	890	His was the English pluck, and there is no tougher or truer, and never was, and never will be,
LG1856	891	Along the lowered eve he came, horribly raking us.
LG1856	892	We closed with him, the yards entangled, the can-noncannon touched,
LG1856	893	My captain lashed fast with his own hands.
LG1856	894	We had received some eighteen-pound shots un-derunder the water,
LG1856	895	On our lower-gun-deck two large pieces had burst at the first fire, killing all around and blowing up overhead.
LG1856	896	Ten o'clock at night and the full moon shining, and the leaks on the gain, and five feet of water reported,
LG1856	897	The master-at-arms loosing the prisoners confined in the after-hold, to give them a chance for themselves.

LG1856	900	Our frigate was afire, the other asked if we de-mandeddemanded quarter? if our colors were struck and the fighting done?

LG1856	930	O Christ! My fit is mastering me!
LG1856	931	What the rebel said, gaily adjusting his throat to the rope-noose,
LG1856	932	What the savage at the stump, his eye-sockets empty, his mouth spirting whoops and defi-ancedefiance,
LG1856	933	What stills the traveler come to the vault at Mount Vernon,

LG1856	935	What burnt the gums of the red-coat at Saratoga when he surrendered his brigades,
LG1856	936	These become mine and me every one, and they are but little,
LG1856	937	I become as much more as I like.
LG1856	938	I become any presence or truth of humanity here,
LG1856	939	And see myself in prison shaped like another man,
LG1856	940	And feel the dull unintermitted pain.
LG1856	941	For me the keepers of convicts shoulder their carbines and keep watch,
LG1856	942	It is I let out in the morning and barred at night.
LG1856	943	Not a mutineer walks hand-cuffed to the jail, but I am hand-cuffed to him and walk by his side,
LG1856	944	I am less the jolly one there, and more the silent one, with sweat on my twitching lips.
LG1856	945	Not a youngster is taken for larceny, but I go up too, and am tried and sentenced.
LG1856	946	Not a cholera patient lies at the last gasp, but I also lie at the last gasp,
LG1856	947	My face is ash-colored, my sinews gnarl, away from me people retreat.
LG1856	948	Askers embody themselves in me, and I am em-bodiedembodied in them,
LG1856	949	I project my hat, sit shame-faced, beg.
LG1856	950	I rise extatic through all, sweep with the true gravitation,
LG1856	951	The whirling and whirling is elemental within me.
LG1856	952	Somehow I have been stunned. Stand back!
LG1856	953	Give me a little time beyond my cuffed head, slumbers, dreams, gaping,
LG1856	954	I discover myself on the verge of a usual mistake.
LG1856	955	That I could forget the mockers and insults!
LG1856	956	That I could forget the trickling tears, and the blows of the bludgeons and hammers!
LG1856	957	That I could look with a separate look on my own crucifixion and bloody crowning!
LG1856	958	I remember, I resume the overstaid fraction,
LG1856	959	The grave of rock multiplies what has been con-fidedconfided to it, or to any graves,
LG1856	960	The corpses rise, the gashes heal, the fastenings roll away.
LG1856	961	I troop forth replenished with supreme power, one of an average unending procession,
LG1856	962	We walk the roads of Ohio, Massachusetts, Vir-giniaVirginia, Wisconsin, Manhattan Island, New Orleans, Texas, Montreal, San Francisco, Charleston, Havana, Mexico,
LG1856	963	Inland and by the sea-coast and boundary lines, and we pass all boundary lines.
LG1856	964	Our swift ordinances are on their way over the whole earth,
LG1856	965	The blossoms we wear in our hats are the growth of two thousand years.
LG1856	966	Eleves, I salute you!
LG1856	967	I see the approach of your numberless gangs, I see you understand yourselves and me,
LG1856	968	And know that they who have eyes are divine, and the blind and lame are equally divine,
LG1856	969	And that my steps drag behind yours, yet go be-forebefore them,
LG1856	970	And are aware how I am with you no more than I am with everybody.
LG1856	971	The friendly and flowing savage, Who is he?
LG1856	972	Is he waiting for civilization, or past it and mas-teringmastering it?
LG1856	973	Is he some south-westerner, raised out-doors? Is he Canadian?
LG1856	974	Is he from the Mississippi country? from Iowa, Oregon, California? from the mountains? prairie-life, bush-life? from the sea?
LG1856	975	Wherever he goes men and women accept and desire him;
LG1856	976	They desire he should like them, touch them speak to them, stay with them.
LG1856	977	Behaviour lawless as snow-flakes, words simple as grass, uncombed head, laughter, naivete,

LG1856	1291	I do not know it—it is without name—it is a word unsaid,
LG1856	1292	It is not in any dictionary, utterance, symbol.
LG1856	1293	Something it swings on more than the earth I swing on,
LG1856	1294	To it the creation is the friend whose embracing awakes me.
LG1856	1295	Perhaps I might tell more. Outlines! I plead for my brothers and sisters.
LG1856	1296	Do you see, O my brothers and sisters?
LG1856	1297	It is not chaos or death—it is form, union, plan —it is eternal life—it is happiness.
LG1856	1298	The past and present wilt—I have filled them, emptied them,
LG1856	1299	And proceed to fill my next fold of the future.
LG1856	1300	Listener up there! here you! what have you to confide to me?
LG1856	1301	Look in my face while I snuff the sidle of evening,
LG1856	1302	Talk honestly, no one else hears you, and I stay only a minute longer.
LG1856	1303	Do I contradict myself?
LG1856	1304	Very well then, I contradict myself,
LG1856	1305	I am large, I contain multitudes.
LG1856	1306	I concentrate toward them that are nigh, I wait on the door-slab.
LG1856	1307	Who has done his day's work? who will soonest be through with his supper?
LG1856	1308	Who wishes to walk with me?
LG1856	1309	Will you speak before I am gone? will you prove already too late?
LG1856	1310	The spotted hawk swoops by and accuses me — he complains of my gab and my loitering.
LG1856	1311	I too am not a bit tamed—I too am untrans-latableuntranslatable,
LG1856	1312	I sound my barbaric yawp over the roofs of the world.
LG1856	1313	The last scud of day holds back for me,
LG1856	1314	It flings my likeness, after the rest, and true as any, on the shadowed wilds,
LG1856	1315	It coaxes me to the vapor and the dusk.
LG1856	1316	I depart as air, I shake my white locks at the run-away sun,
LG1856	1317	I effuse my flash in eddies, and drift it in lacy jags.
LG1856	1318	I bequeath myself to the dirt, to grow from the grass I love,
LG1856	1319	If you want me again, look for me under your boot-soles.
LG1856	1320	You will hardly know who I am, or what I mean,
LG1856	1321	But I shall be good health to you nevertheless,
LG1856	1322	And filter and fibre your blood.
LG1856	1323	Failing to fetch me at first, keep encouraged,
LG1856	1324	Missing me one place, search another,
LG1856	1325	I stop some where waiting for you.
LG1856	1326	UNFOLDED only out of the folds of the woman, man comes unfolded, and is always to come unfolded,
LG1856	1327	Unfolded only out of the superbest woman of the earth is to come the superbest man of the earth,
LG1856	1328	Unfolded out of the friendliest woman is to come the friendliest man,
LG1856	1329	Unfolded only out of the perfect body of a woman, can a man be formed of perfect body,
LG1856	1330	Unfolded only out of the inimitable poem of the woman can come the poems of man — only thence have my poems come,
LG1856	1331	Unfolded out of the strong and arrogant woman I love, only thence can appear the strong and arrogant man I love,
LG1856	1332	Unfolded by brawny embraces from the well-muscledwell-muscled woman I love, only thence come the brawny embraces of the man,
LG1856	1333	Unfolded out of the folds of the woman's brain, come all the folds of the man's brain, duly obedient,

LG1856	1335	Unfolded out of the sympathy of the woman is all sympathy;
LG1856	1336	A man is a great thing upon the earth, and through eternity—but every jot of the great-nessgreatness of man is unfolded out of woman,
LG1856	1337	First the man is shaped in the woman, he can then be shaped in himself.
LG1856	1338	O TAKE my hand, Walt Whitman!
LG1856	1339	Such gliding wonders! Such sights and sounds!
LG1856	1340	Such joined unended links, each hooked to the next!
LG1856	1341	Each answering all, each sharing the earth with all.
LG1856	1342	What widens within you, Walt Whitman?

LG1856	1588	I find my home wherever there are any homes of men.
LG1856	1589	COME closer to me,
LG1856	1590	Push close, my lovers, and take the best I possess,
LG1856	1591	Yield closer and closer, and give me the best you possess.
LG1856	1592	This is unfinished business with me—How is it with you?
LG1856	1593	I was chilled with the cold types, cylinder, wet paper between us.
LG1856	1594	I pass so poorly with paper and types, I must pass with the contact of bodies and souls.
LG1856	1595	I do not thank you for liking me as I am, and liking the touch of me—I know that it is good for you to do so.
LG1856	1596	Were all educations practical and ornamental well displayed out of me, what would it amount to?
LG1856	1597	Were I as the head teacher, charitable proprietor, wise statesman, what would it amount to?
LG1856	1598	Were I to you as the boss employing and paying you, would that satisfy you?
LG1856	1599	The learned, virtuous, benevolent, and the usual terms,
LG1856	1600	A man like me, and never the usual terms.
LG1856	1601	Neither a servant nor a master am I,
LG1856	1602	I take no sooner a large price than a small price —I will have my own, whoever enjoys me,
LG1856	1603	I will be even with you, and you shall be even with me.
LG1856	1604	If you are a workman or workwoman, I stand as nigh as the nighest that works in the same shop,
LG1856	1605	If you bestow gifts on your brother or dearest friend, I demand as good as your brother or dearest friend,
LG1856	1606	If your lover, husband, wife, is welcome by day or night, I must be personally as welcome,
LG1856	1607	If you become degraded, criminal, ill, then I become so for your sake,
LG1856	1608	If you remember your foolish and outlawed deeds, do you think I cannot remember my own foolish and outlawed deeds? plenty of them?
LG1856	1609	If you carouse at the table, I carouse at the opposite side of the table,
LG1856	1610	If you meet some stranger in the street, and love him or her, do I not often meet strangers in the street and love them?
LG1856	1611	If you see a good deal remarkable in me, I see just as much, perhaps more, in you.
LG1856	1612	Why what have you thought of yourself?
LG1856	1613	Is it you, then, that thought yourself less?
LG1856	1614	Is it you that thought the President greater than you? or the rich better off than you? or the educated wiser than you?
LG1856	1615	Because you are greasy or pimpled, or that you was once drunk, or a thief, or diseased, or rheumatic, or a prostitute, or are so now, or from frivolity or impotence, or that you are no scholar, and never saw your name in print, do you give in that you are any less immortal?
LG1856	1616	Souls of men and women! it is not you I call unseen, unheard, untouchable and untouch-inguntouching,
LG1856	1617	It is not you I go argue pro and con about, and to settle whether you are alive or no,
LG1856	1618	I own publicly who you are, if nobody else owns —I see and hear you, and what you give and take,
LG1856	1619	What is there you cannot give and take?
LG1856	1620	I see not merely that you are polite or white-faced, married, single, citizens of old states, citizens of new states, eminent in some profession, a lady or gentleman in a parlor, or dressed in the jail uniform, or pulpit uniform,
LG1856	1621	Not only the free Utahan, Kansian, Arkansian — not only the free Cuban, not merely the slave, not Mexican native, Flatfoot, negro from Africa,
LG1856	1622	Iroquois eating the war-flesh, fish-tearer in his lair of rocks and sand, Esquimaux in the dark cold snow-house, Chinese with his transverse eyes, Bedowee, wandering nomad, taboun-schiktabounschik at the head of his droves,
LG1856	1623	Grown, half-grown, and babe, of this country and every country, indoors and outdoors, I see — and all else is behind or through them.
LG1856	1624	The wife, and she is not one jot less than the husband!
LG1856	1625	The daughter, and she is just as good as the son!
LG1856	1626	The mother, and she is every bit as much as the father!
LG1856	1627	Offspring of those not rich, boys apprenticed to trades,
LG1856	1628	Young fellows working on farms, and old fellows working on farms,
LG1856	1629	The naive, the simple and hardy, he going to the polls to vote, he who has a good time, and he who has a bad time,
LG1856	1630	Mechanics, southerners, new arrivals, laborers sailors, mano'warsmen, merchantmen, coast-erscoasters,
LG1856	1631	All these I see, but nigher and farther the same I see,
LG1856	1632	None shall escape me, and none shall wish to escape me.
LG1856	1633	I bring what you much need, yet always have,
LG1856	1634	Not money, amours, dress, eating, but as good,
LG1856	1635	I send no agent or medium, offer no representative of value, but offer the value itself.
LG1856	1636	There is something that comes home to one now and perpetually,
LG1856	1637	It is not what is printed, preached, discussed—it eludes discussion and print,
LG1856	1638	It is not to be put in a book, it is not in this book,
LG1856	1639	It is for you, whoever you are—it is no farther from you than your hearing and sight are from you,
LG1856	1640	It is hinted by nearest, commonest, readiest—it is not them, though it is endlessly provoked by them—what is there ready and near you now?
LG1856	1641	You may read in many languages, yet read nothing about it,
LG1856	1642	You may read the President's message, and read nothing about it there,
LG1856	1643	Nothing in the reports from the State department or Treasury department, or in the daily papers or the weekly papers,
LG1856	1644	Or in the census returns, assessors' returns, prices current, or any accounts of stock.
LG1856	1645	The sun and stars that float in the open air—the apple-shaped earth, and we upon it, surely the drift of them is something grand!
LG1856	1646	I do not know what it is, except that it is grand, and that it is happiness,
LG1856	1647	And that the enclosing purport of us here is not a speculation, or bon-mot, or recon-noissancereconnoissance,
LG1856	1648	And that it is not something which by luck may turn out well for us, and without luck must be a failure for us,
LG1856	1649	And not something which may yet be retracted in a certain contingency.
LG1856	1650	The light and shade, the curious sense of body and identity, the greed that with perfect complaisance devours all things, the endless pride and out-stretching of man, unspeakable joys and sorrows,
LG1856	1651	The wonder every one sees in every one else he sees, and the wonders that fill each minute of time forever, and each acre of surface and space forever,
LG1856	1652	Have you reckoned them for a trade or farm-work? or for the profits of a store? or to achieve yourself a position? or to fill a gentleman's leisure, or a lady's leisure?
LG1856	1653	Have you reckoned the landscape took substance and form that it might be painted in a picture?
LG1856	1654	Or men and women that they might be written of, and songs sung?
LG1856	1655	Or the attraction of gravity, and the great laws and harmonious combinations, and the fluids of the air, as subjects for the savans?

LG1856	1661	I rate them high as the highest, then a child born of a woman and man I rate beyond all rate.

LG1856	1663	I do not say they are not grand and good, for they are,

LG1856	1879	Muscle and pluck forever!

LG1856	1885	What do you think endures?
LG1856	1886	Do you think the greatest city endures?
LG1856	1887	Or a teeming manufacturing state? or a prepared constitution? or the best built steam-ships?

LG1856	1893	The greatest city is that which has the greatest man or woman,
LG1856	1894	If it be a few ragged huts, it is still the greatest city in the whole world.
LG1856	1895	The place where the greatest city stands is not the place of stretched wharves, docks, manu-facturesmanufactures, deposites of produce,

LG1856	1900	Where the city stands with the brawniest breed of orators and bards,
LG1856	1901	Where the city stands that is beloved by these, and loves them in return, and understands them,
LG1856	1902	Where these may be seen going every day in the streets, with their arms familiar to the shoul-dersshoulders of their friends,
LG1856	1903	Where no monuments exist to heroes but in the common words and deeds,

LG1856	1907	Where the slave ceases and the master of slaves ceases,

LG1856	1956	I see the European headsman,

LG1856	2038	She, less guarded than ever, yet more guarded than ever,

LG1856	2076	GREAT are the myths, I too delight in them,

LG1856	2734	I loved well the stately and rapid river,
LG1856	2735	The men and women I saw were all near to me,
LG1856	2736	Others the same—others who look back on me, because I looked forward to them,
LG1856	2737	The time will come, though I stop here today and tonight.
LG1856	2738	What is it, then, between us? What is the count of the scores or hundreds of years between us?

LG1856	2740	I too lived,
LG1856	2741	I too walked the streets of Manhattan Island, and bathed in the waters around it;
LG1856	2742	I too felt the curious abrupt questionings stir with-inwithin me,

LG1856	2746	I too had received identity by my body,
LG1856	2747	That I was, I knew was of my body, and what I should be, I knew I should be of my body.

LG1856	2749	The dark threw patches down upon me also,
LG1856	2750	The best I had done seemed to me blank and sus-picioussuspicious,

LG1856	2754	I too knitted the old knot of contrariety,
LG1856	2755	Blabbed, blushed, resented, lied, stole, grudged,
LG1856	2756	Had guile, anger, lust, hot wishes I dared not speak,

LG1856	3041	Have the past struggles succeeded?
LG1856	3042	What has succeeded? Yourself? Your nation? Nature?
LG1856	3043	Now understand me well—it is provided in the essence of things, that from any fruition of success, no matter what, shall come forth something to make a greater struggle neces-sarynecessary.

LG1856	3045	He going with me must go well armed,
LG1856	3046	He going with me goes often with spare diet, poverty, angry enemies, contentions.
LG1856	3047	Allons! the road is before us!
LG1856	3048	It is safe—I have tried it—my own feet have tried it well.
LG1856	3049	Allons! be not detained!
LG1856	3050	Let the paper remain on the desk unwritten, and the book on the shelf unopened!

LG1856	3054	Mon enfant! I give you my hand!
LG1856	3055	I give you my love, more precious than money,
LG1856	3056	I give you myself, before preaching or law;
LG1856	3057	Will you give me yourself? Will you come travel with me?
LG1856	3058	Shall we stick by each other as long as we live?
LG1856	3059	A WOMAN waits for me—she contains all, nothing is lacking,
LG1856	3060	Yet all were lacking, if sex were lacking, or if the moisture of the right man were lacking.

LG1856	3071	They cannot be fetched, I say, on less terms than mine,

LG1856	3079	Their flesh has the old divine suppleness and strength,

LG1856	3082	I draw you close to me, you women!
LG1856	3083	I cannot let you go, I would do you good,
LG1856	3084	I am for you, and you are for me, not only for our own sake, but for others' sakes,
LG1856	3085	Enveloped in you sleep greater heroes and bards,
LG1856	3086	They refuse to awake at the touch of any man but me.
LG1856	3087	It is I, you women—I make my way,
LG1856	3088	I am stern, acrid, large, undissuadable—but I love you,
LG1856	3089	I do not hurt you any more than is necessary for you,
LG1856	3090	I pour the stuff to start sons and daughters fit for These States—I press with slow rude muscle,

LG1856	3093	Through you I drain the pent-up rivers of myself,
LG1856	3094	In you I wrap a thousand onward years,

LG1856	3101	I shall look for loving crops from the birth, life, death, immortality I plant so lovingly now.
LG1856	3102	A YOUNG man came to me with a message from his brother,
LG1856	3103	How should the young man know the whether and when of his brother?
LG1856	3104	Tell him to send me the signs.
LG1856	3105	And I stood before the young man face to face, and took his right hand in my left hand, and his left hand in my right hand,
LG1856	3106	And I answered for his brother, and for men, and I answered for the poet, and sent these signs.
LG1856	3107	Him all wait for, him all yield up to, his word is decisive and final,
LG1856	3108	Him they accept, in him lave, in him perceive themselves, as amid light,
LG1856	3109	Him they immerse, and he immerses them.
LG1856	3110	Beautiful women, the haughtiest nations, laws, the landscape, people, animals,
LG1856	3111	The profound earth and its attributes, and the un-quietunquiet ocean,

LG1856	3114	The noblest and costliest cities, others grading and building, and he domiciles there,

LG1856	3117	He puts things in their attitudes,
LG1856	3118	He puts today out of himself, with plasticity and love,
LG1856	3119	He places his own city, times, reminiscences, parents, brothers and sisters, associations, employment, politics, so that the rest never shame them afterward, nor assume to com-mandcommand them.
LG1856	3120	He is the answerer,
LG1856	3121	What can be answered he answers, and what cannot be answered, he shows how it cannot be answered.
LG1856	3122	A man is a summons and challenge;
LG1856	3123	It is vain to skulk—Do you hear that mocking and laughter? Do you hear the ironical echoes?
LG1856	3124	Books, friendships, philosophers, priests, action, pleasure, pride, beat up and down, seeking to give satisfaction,

LG1856	3126	Whichever the sex, whatever the season or place, he may go freshly and gently and safely, by day or by night,
LG1856	3127	He has the pass-key of hearts—to him the response of the prying of hands on the knobs.
LG1856	3128	His welcome is universal—the flow of beauty is not more welcome or universal than he is,
LG1856	3129	The person he favors by day or sleeps with at night is blessed.
LG1856	3130	Every existence has its idiom, every thing has an idiom and tongue,
LG1856	3131	He resolves all tongues into his own, and bestows it upon men, and any man translates, and any man translates himself also,
LG1856	3132	One part does not counteract another part—he is the joiner, he sees how they join.
LG1856	3133	He says indifferently and alike, How are you, friend? to the President at his levee,
LG1856	3134	And he says, Good-day, my brother! to Cudge that hoes in the sugar-field,

LG1856	3139	And the soldiers suppose him to be a captain, and the sailors that he has followed the sea,

LG1856	3141	And the laborers perceive he could labor with them and love them.
LG1856	3142	No matter what the work is, that he is the one to follow it, or has followed it,
LG1856	3143	No matter what the nation, that he might find his brothers and sisters there.

LG1856	3152	Do you think it would be good to be the writer of melodious verses?

LG1856	3156	THIS night I am happy,
LG1856	3157	As I watch the stars shining, I think a thought of the clef of the universes, and of the future.
LG1856	3158	What can the future bring me more than I have?
LG1856	3159	Do you suppose I wish to enjoy life in other spheres?
LG1856	3160	I say distinctly I comprehend no better sphere than this earth,
LG1856	3161	I comprehend no better life than the life of my body.
LG1856	3162	I do not know what follows the death of my body,

LG1856	3189	SUDDENLY out of its stale and drowsy lair, the lair of slaves,

LG1856	3192	O hope and faith! O aching close of lives! O many a sickened heart!

LG1856	3194	And you, paid to defile the People! you liars, mark!

LG1856	3243	The soul is of itself,
LG1856	3244	All verges to it, all has reference to what ensues,

LG1856	3292	PERFECT sanity shows the master among philosophs,

LG1856	3308	The sailor and traveler underlie the maker of poems,

LG1856	3310	The words of poems give you more than poems,
LG1856	3311	They give you to form for yourself poems, religions, politics, war, peace, behaviour, histories, essays, romances, and every thing else,

LG1856	3318	I NEED no assurances—I am a man who is pre-occupied of his own soul;
LG1856	3319	I do not doubt that whatever I know at a given time, there waits for me more which I do not know;
LG1856	3320	I do not doubt that from under the feet, and beside the hands and face I am cognizant of, are now looking faces I am not cognizant of — calm and actual faces;
LG1856	3321	I do not doubt but the majesty and beauty of the world is latent in any iota of the world;
LG1856	3322	I do not doubt there are realizations I have no idea of, waiting for me through time and through the universes—also upon this earth;
LG1856	3323	I do not doubt I am limitless, and that the uni-versesuniverses are limitless—in vain I try to think how limitless;
LG1856	3324	I do not doubt that the orbs, and the systems of orbs, play their swift sports through the air 12 on purpose—and that I shall one day be eligible to do as much as they, and more than they;
LG1856	3325	I do not doubt there is far more in trivialities, insects, vulgar persons, slaves, dwarfs, weeds, rejected refuse, than I have supposed;
LG1856	3326	I do not doubt there is more in myself than I have supposed—and more in all men and women —and more in my poems than I have supposed;
LG1856	3327	I do not doubt that temporary affairs keep on and on, millions of years;
LG1856	3328	I do not doubt interiors have their interiors, and exteriors have their exteriors—and that the eye-sight has another eye-sight, and the hear-inghearing another hearing, and the voice another voice;
LG1856	3329	I do not doubt that the passionately-wept deaths of young men are provided for—and that the deaths of young women, and the deaths of little children, are provided for;
LG1856	3330	I do not doubt that wrecks at sea, no matter what the horrors of them—no matter whose wife, child, husband, father, lover, has gone down—are provided for, to the minutest point;
LG1856	3331	I do not doubt that shallowness, meanness, malig-nancemalignance, are provided for;
LG1856	3332	I do not doubt that cities, you, America, the remainder of the earth, politics, freedom, degradations, are carefully provided for;
LG1856	3333	I do not doubt that whatever can possibly happen, any where, at any time, is provided for, in the inherences of things.
LG1856	3334	COURAGE! my brother or my sister!
LG1856	3335	Keep on! Liberty is to be subserved, what-everwhatever occurs;
LG1856	3336	That is nothing, that is quelled by one or two fail-uresfailures, or any number of failures,
LG1856	3337	Or by the indifference or ingratitude of the people,
LG1856	3338	Or the show of the tushes of power—soldiers, cannon, penal statutes.
LG1856	3339	What we believe in waits latent forever through Asia, Africa, Europe, America, Australia, Cuba, and all the islands and archipelagoes of the sea;
LG1856	3340	What we believe in invites no one, promises nothing, sits in calmness and light, is positive and composed, knows no discouragement,
LG1856	3341	Waits patiently its time—a year—a century — a hundred centuries.
LG1856	3342	The battle rages with many a loud alarm and frequent advance and retreat,
LG1856	3343	The infidel triumphs—or supposes he triumphs,
LG1856	3344	The prison, scaffold, garrote, hand-cuffs, iron neck-lacenecklace and anklet, lead-balls, do their work,
LG1856	3345	The named and unnamed heroes pass to other spheres,
LG1856	3346	The great speakers and writers are exiled—they lie sick in distant lands,
LG1856	3347	The cause is asleep—the strong throats are choked with their own blood,
LG1856	3348	The young men drop their eye-lashes toward the ground when they meet,

LG1856	3350	When liberty goes out of a place, it is not the first to go, nor the second or third to go,
LG1856	3351	It waits for all the rest to go—it is the last.
LG1856	3352	When there are no more memories of the lovers of the whole of the nations of the world,
LG1856	3353	The lovers' names scouted in the public gatherings by the lips of the orators,
LG1856	3354	Boys not christened after them, but christened after traitors and murderers instead,
LG1856	3355	Laws for slaves sweet to the taste of people — the slave-hunt acknowledged,
