LG1856	3359	Then shall the infidel and the tyrant come into possession.
LG1856	3360	CLEAR the way there, Jonathan!
LG1856	3361	Way for the President's marshal! Way for the government cannon!
LG1856	3362	Way for the federal foot and dragoons—and the apparitions copiously tumbling.
LG1856	3363	I rose this morning early to get betimes in Boston town,
LG1856	3364	Here's a good place at the corner, I must stand and see the show.
LG1856	3365	I love to look on the stars and stripes, I hope the fifes will play Yankee Doodle.
LG1856	3366	How bright shine the cutlasses of the foremost troops!
LG1856	3367	Every man holds his revolver, marching stiff through Boston town.

LG1856	3369	Some appear wooden-legged and some appear bandaged and bloodless.

LG1856	3371	The old grave-yards of the hills have hurried to see!

LG1856	3401	REMEMBER the organic compact of These States!

LG1856	3444	REALISM is mine, my miracles,
LG1856	3445	Take all of the rest—take freely—I keep but my own—I give only of them,

LG1856	3447	Why! who makes much of a miracle?
LG1856	3448	As to me, I know of nothing else but miracles,
LG1856	3449	Whether I walk the streets of Manhattan,
LG1856	3450	Or dart my sight over the roofs of houses toward the sky,

LG1856	3453	Or talk by day with any one I love—or sleep in the bed at night with any one I love,

LG1856	3468	These, with the rest, one and all, are to me miracles,
LG1856	3469	The whole referring—yet each distinct and in its place.

LG1856	3472	Every square yard of the surface of the earth is spread with the same,

LG1856	3477	The fishes that swim—the rocks—the motion of the waves—the ships, with men in them —what stranger miracles are there?
LG1856	3478	THERE was a child went forth every day,
LG1856	3479	And the first object he looked upon and re-ceivedreceived with wonder, pity, love, or dread, that object he became,
LG1856	3480	And that object became part of him for the day, or a certain part of the day, or for many years, or stretching cycles of years.
LG1856	3481	The early lilacs became part of this child,
LG1856	3482	And grass, and white and red morning-glories, and white and red clover, and the song of the phœbe-bird,
LG1856	3483	And the March-born lambs, and the sow's pink-faintpink-faint litter, and the mare's foal, and the cow's calf, and the noisy brood of the barn-yard or by the mire of the pond-side, and the fish suspending themselves so curiously below there, and the beautiful curious liquid, and the water-plants with their graceful flat heads — all became part of him.
LG1856	3484	The field-sprouts of April and May became part of him—winter-grain sprouts, and those of the light-yellow corn, and of the esculent roots of the garden,
LG1856	3485	And the apple-trees covered with blossoms, and the fruit afterward, and wood-berries, and the commonest weeds by the road,
LG1856	3486	And the old drunkard staggering home from the out-house of the tavern whence he had lately risen,

LG1856	3488	And all the changes of city and country, wherever he went.
LG1856	3489	His own parents—he that had propelled the father-stuff at night and fathered him, and she that conceived him in her womb and birthed him—they gave this child more of themselves than that,
LG1856	3490	They gave him afterward every day—they and of them became part of him.
LG1856	3491	The mother at home, quietly placing the dishes on the supper-table,
LG1856	3492	The mother with mild words, clean her cap and gown, a wholesome odor falling off her per-sonperson and clothes as she walks by,

LG1856	3496	Affection that will not be gainsayed—the sense of what is real—the thought if, after all, it should prove unreal,

LG1856	3500	The streets themselves, and the facades of houses, the goods in the windows,

LG1856	3510	I WANDER all night in my vision,
LG1856	3511	Stepping with light feet, swiftly and noise-lesslynoiselessly stepping and stopping,
LG1856	3512	Bending with open eyes over the shut eyes of sleepers,
LG1856	3513	Wandering and confused, lost to myself, ill-assortedill-assorted, contradictory,

LG1856	3515	How solemn they look there, stretched and still!
LG1856	3516	How quiet they breathe, the little children in their cradles!
LG1856	3517	The wretched features of ennuyees, the white features of corpses, the livid faces of drunk-ardsdrunkards, the sick-gray faces of onanists,

LG1856	3521	The married couple sleep calmly in their bed — he with his palm on the hip of the wife, and she with her palm on the hip of the husband,

LG1856	3523	The men sleep lovingly side by side in theirs,
LG1856	3524	And the mother sleeps with her little child care-fullycarefully wrapped.

LG1856	3528	And the murdered person—how does he sleep?
LG1856	3529	The female that loves unrequited sleeps,
LG1856	3530	And the male that loves unrequited sleeps;
LG1856	3531	The head of the money-maker that plotted all day sleeps,
LG1856	3532	And the enraged and treacherous dispositions sleep.
LG1856	3533	I stand with drooping eyes by the worst-suffering and restless,
LG1856	3534	I pass my hands soothingly to and fro a few inches from them,
LG1856	3535	The restless sink in their beds—they fitfully sleep.
LG1856	3536	The earth recedes from me into the night,
LG1856	3537	I saw that it was beautiful, and I see that what is not the earth is beautiful.
LG1856	3538	I go from bedside to bedside, I sleep close with the other sleepers, each in turn,
LG1856	3539	I dream in my dream all the dreams of the other dreamers,
LG1856	3540	And I become the other dreamers.
LG1856	3541	I am a dance—Play up, there! the fit is whirling me fast!
LG1856	3542	I am the ever-laughing—it is new moon and twilight,
LG1856	3543	I see the hiding of douceurs, I see nimble ghosts whichever way I look,
LG1856	3544	Cache, and cache again, deep in the ground and sea, and where it is neither ground or sea.
LG1856	3545	Well do they do their jobs, those journeymen divine,
LG1856	3546	Only from me can they hide nothing, and would not if they could,
LG1856	3547	I reckon I am their boss, and they make me a pet besides,
LG1856	3548	And surround me and lead me, and run ahead when I walk,
LG1856	3549	To lift their cunning covers, to signify me with stretched arms, and resume the way;

LG1856	3551	I am the actor, the actress, the voter, the poli-ticianpolitician,

LG1856	3555	I am she who adorned herself and folded her hair expectantly,
LG1856	3556	My truant lover has come, and it is dark.
LG1856	3557	Double yourself and receive me, darkness!
LG1856	3558	Receive me and my lover too—he will not let me go without him.
LG1856	3559	I roll myself upon you, as upon a bed—I resign myself to the dusk.
LG1856	3560	He whom I call answers me and takes the place of my lover,

LG1856	3562	Darkness, you are gentler than my lover! his flesh was sweaty and panting,

LG1856	3564	My hands are spread forth, I pass them in all directions,
LG1856	3565	I would sound up the shadowy shore to which you are journeying.
LG1856	3566	Be careful, darkness! already, what was it touched me?
LG1856	3567	I thought my lover had gone, else darkness and he are one,
LG1856	3568	I hear the heart-beat, I follow, I fade away.
LG1856	3569	O hot-cheeked and blushing! O foolish hectic!
LG1856	3570	O for pity's sake, no one must see me now! my clothes were stolen while I was abed,
LG1856	3571	Now I am thrust forth, where shall I run?
LG1856	3572	Pier that I saw dimly last night, when I looked from the windows!
LG1856	3573	Pier out from the main, let me catch myself with you and stay! I will not chafe you,
LG1856	3574	I feel ashamed to go naked about the world.
LG1856	3575	I am curious to know where my feet stand—and what this is flooding me, childhood or man-hoodmanhood—and the hunger that crosses the bridge between.
LG1856	3576	The cloth laps a first sweet eating and drinking,
LG1856	3577	Laps life-swelling yolks—laps ear of rose-corn, milky and just ripened;
LG1856	3578	The white teeth stay, and the boss-tooth advances in darkness,

LG1856	3581	Perfume and youth course through me, and I am their wake.
LG1856	3582	It is my face yellow and wrinkled, instead of the old woman's,
LG1856	3583	I sit low in a straw-bottom chair, and carefully darn my grand-son's stockings.
LG1856	3584	It is I too, the sleepless widow looking out on the winter midnight,
LG1856	3585	I see the sparkles of starshine on the icy and pallid earth.
LG1856	3586	A shroud I see, and I am the shroud—I wrap a body and lie in the coffin,
LG1856	3587	It is dark here underground, it is not evil or pain here, it is blank here, for reasons.
LG1856	3588	It seems to me that everything in the light and air ought to be happy,
LG1856	3589	Whoever is not in his coffin and the dark grave, let him know he has enough.
LG1856	3590	I see a beautiful gigantic swimmer swimming naked through the eddies of the sea,
LG1856	3591	His brown hair lies close and even to his head, he strikes out with courageous arms, he urges himself with his legs,
LG1856	3592	I see his white body, I see his undaunted eyes,
LG1856	3593	I hate the swift-running eddies that would dash him head-foremost on the rocks.
LG1856	3594	What are you doing, you ruffianly red-trickled waves?
LG1856	3595	Will you kill the courageous giant? Will you kill him in the prime of his middle age?
LG1856	3596	Steady and long he struggles,
LG1856	3597	He is baffled, banged, bruised—he holds out while his strength holds out,
LG1856	3598	The slapping eddies are spotted with his blood — they bear him away, they roll him, swing him, turn him,
LG1856	3599	His beautiful body is borne in the circling eddies, it is continually bruised on rocks,
LG1856	3600	Swiftly and out of sight is borne the brave corpse.
LG1856	3601	I turn, but do not extricate myself,
LG1856	3602	Confused, a past-reading, another, but with dark-nessdarkness yet.
LG1856	3603	The beach is cut by the razory ice-wind, the wreck-guns sound,

LG1856	3605	I look where the ship helplessly heads end on—I hear the burst as she strikes—I hear the howls of dismay—they grow fainter and fainter.
LG1856	3606	I cannot aid with my wringing fingers,
LG1856	3607	I can but rush to the surf, and let it drench me and freeze upon me.
LG1856	3608	I search with the crowd—not one of the company is washed to us alive;
LG1856	3609	In the morning I help pick up the dead and lay them in rows in a barn.
LG1856	3610	Now of the old war-days, the defeat at Brooklyn,

LG1856	3619	Now I tell what my mother told me today as we sat at dinner together,
LG1856	3620	Of when she was a nearly grown girl living home with her parents on the old homestead.
LG1856	3621	A red squaw came one breakfast-time to the old homestead,
LG1856	3622	On her back she carried a bundle of rushes for rush-bottoming chairs,
LG1856	3623	Her hair, straight, shiny, coarse, black, profuse, half-enveloped her face,
LG1856	3624	Her step was free and elastic, her voice sounded exquisitely as she spoke.
